
\medterm{C-Peptide} A peptide released in equimolar amounts with endogenous insulin when proinsulin is
cleaved. Measurement helps estimate endogenous beta-cell insulin secretion and
distinguish endogenous from injected insulin, with interpretation affected by renal
function and glucose level.

\medterm{C. diff} Alternate index label; see \textit{C. difficile infection}.

\medterm{C. difficile colitis} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pseudomembranous colitis}.

\medterm{Cancer of unknown origin} Alternate index label; see \textit{Carcinoma of unknown primary}.

\medterm{Cancer, carcinoid tumors} Alternate index label; see \textit{Carcinoid tumors}.

\medterm{Cancer, chronic lymphocytic leukemia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic lymphocytic leukemia}.

\medterm{Cancer, chronic myelogenous leukemia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic myelogenous leukemia}.

\medterm{Cancer, colon} Alternate index label; see \textit{Colon cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, endometrial} Alternate index label; see \textit{Endometrial cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, esophageal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Esophageal cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, eye melanoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Eye melanoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, floor of the mouth} Alternate index label; see \textit{Floor of the mouth cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, gallbladder} Alternate index label; see \textit{Gallbladder cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, gastric} Alternate index label; see \textit{Stomach cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, hairy cell leukemia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hairy cell leukemia}.

\medterm{Cancer, Hodgkin lymphoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hodgkin lymphoma (Hodgkin disease)}.

\medterm{Cancer, Hurthle cell} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hurthle cell cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, inflammatory breast} Alternate index label; see \textit{Inflammatory breast cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, kidney} Alternate index label; see \textit{Kidney cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, liver} Alternate index label; see \textit{Liver cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, lung} Alternate index label; see \textit{Lung cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, Merkel cell} Alternate index label; see \textit{Merkel cell carcinoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, mouth} Alternate index label; see \textit{Mouth cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, multiple myeloma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Multiple myeloma}.

\medterm{Cancer, nasopharyngeal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Nasopharyngeal carcinoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, neuroblastoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Neuroblastoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, neuroendocrine} Alternate index label; see \textit{Neuroendocrine tumors}.

\medterm{Cancer, non-Hodgkin lymphoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Non-Hodgkin lymphoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, ovarian} Alternate index label; see \textit{Ovarian cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, pancreatic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pancreatic cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, paraneoplastic syndromes} Alternate index label; see \textit{Paraneoplastic syndromes of the nervous system}.

\medterm{Cancer, prostate} Alternate index label; see \textit{Prostate cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, rectal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Rectal cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, retinoblastoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Retinoblastoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, skin} Alternate index label; see \textit{Skin cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, soft tissue sarcoma} Alternate index label; see \textit{Soft tissue sarcoma}.

\medterm{Cancer, squamous cell} Alternate index label; see \textit{Squamous cell carcinoma of the skin}.

\medterm{Cancer, stomach} Alternate index label; see \textit{Stomach cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, testicular} Alternate index label; see \textit{Testicular cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, throat} Alternate index label; see \textit{Throat cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, thyroid} Alternate index label; see \textit{Thyroid cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, uterine} Alternate index label; see \textit{Endometrial cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, vagina} Alternate index label; see \textit{Vaginal cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, vulvar} Alternate index label; see \textit{Vulvar cancer}.

\medterm{Cancer, Wilms' tumor} Alternate index label; see \textit{Wilms tumor}.

\medterm{Candidiasis, oral} Alternate index label; see \textit{Oral thrush}.

\medterm{Candidiasis, vaginal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Yeast infection (vaginal)}.

\medterm{Carbuncles and boils} Alternate index label; see \textit{Boils and carbuncles}.

\medterm{Cardiac arrest, sudden} Alternate index label; see \textit{Sudden cardiac arrest}.

\medterm{Cardiac ischemia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Myocardial ischemia}.

\medterm{Cardiomyopathy, dilated} Alternate index label; see \textit{Dilated cardiomyopathy}.

\medterm{Cardiomyopathy, hypertrophic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy}.

\medterm{Carotid Arteries} Major paired arteries in the neck supplying blood to the brain, neck, and face; common site for atherosclerotic plaque formation and stenosis.

\medterm{Carotid artery stenosis} Alternate index label; see \textit{Carotid artery disease}.

\medterm{Carotid insufficiency} Alternate index label; see \textit{Carotid artery disease}.

\medterm{CAS} Alternate index label; see \textit{Childhood apraxia of speech}.

\medterm{Central neurofibromatosis} Alternate index label; see \textit{NF2-related schwannomatosis (neurofibromatosis type 2)}.

\medterm{Cercarial dermatitis} Alternate index label; see \textit{Swimmer's itch}.

\medterm{Cerebral embolism} Alternate index label; see \textit{Embolic stroke (brain embolism)}.

\medterm{Cervical osteoarthritis} Alternate index label; see \textit{Cervical spondylosis}.

\medterm{Cervical pain} Alternate index label; see \textit{Neck pain}.

\medterm{CF} Alternate index label; see \textit{Cystic fibrosis}.

\medterm{CHD} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hip dysplasia}.

\medterm{Chemical dependency} Alternate index label; see \textit{Drug addiction (substance use disorder)}.

\medterm{Chest wall pain} Alternate index label; see \textit{Costochondritis}.

\medterm{Child maltreatment} Alternate index label; see \textit{Child abuse}.

\medterm{Cholangitis, primary sclerosing} Alternate index label; see \textit{Primary sclerosing cholangitis (PSC)}.

\medterm{Chorea, Huntington's} Alternate index label; see \textit{Huntington's disease}.

\medterm{Chronic acid reflux} Alternate index label; see \textit{Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD)}.

\medterm{Chronic adrenal insufficiency} Alternate index label; see \textit{Addison's disease}.

\medterm{Chronic compartment syndrome} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic exertional compartment syndrome}.

\medterm{Chronic kidney failure} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic kidney disease}.

\medterm{Chronic lymphocytic thyroiditis} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hashimoto's disease}.

\medterm{Chronic renal failure} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic kidney disease}.

\medterm{Chronic vulvar pain} Alternate index label; see \textit{Vulvodynia}.

\medterm{CJD} Alternate index label; see \textit{Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease}.

\medterm{Click-murmur syndrome} Alternate index label; see \textit{Mitral valve prolapse}.

\medterm{Clinic Visit} Scheduled outpatient appointment for medical consultation, follow-up, or monitoring of a chronic condition.

\medterm{CLL} Alternate index label; see \textit{Chronic lymphocytic leukemia}.

\medterm{Cold allergy} Alternate index label; see \textit{Cold urticaria}.

\medterm{Cold exposure} Alternate index label; see \textit{Hypothermia}.

\medterm{Cold, common} Alternate index label; see \textit{Common cold}.

\medterm{Colitis, ischemic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Ischemic colitis}.

\medterm{Colitis, microscopic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Microscopic colitis}.

\medterm{Colitis, pseudomembranous} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pseudomembranous colitis}.

\medterm{Colitis, ulcerative} Alternate index label; see \textit{Ulcerative colitis}.

\medterm{Collapsed arch} Alternate index label; see \textit{Flatfeet}.

\medterm{Columnar epithelium lined lower esophagus} Alternate index label; see \textit{Barrett's esophagus}.

\medterm{Complicated bereavement} Alternate index label; see \textit{Complicated grief}.

\medterm{Compressed nerve} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pinched nerve}.

\medterm{Compulsive overeating} Alternate index label; see \textit{Binge-eating disorder}.

\medterm{Compulsive stealing} Alternate index label; see \textit{Kleptomania}.

\medterm{Condylomata acuminata} Alternate index label; see \textit{Genital warts}.

\medterm{Congenital biliary atresia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Biliary atresia}.

\medterm{Congenital plagiocephaly} Alternate index label; see \textit{Craniosynostosis}.

\medterm{Conversion disorder} Alternate index label; see \textit{Functional neurologic disorder/conversion disorder}.

\medterm{Cooley's anemia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Thalassemia}.

\medterm{Coronary microvascular disease} Alternate index label; see \textit{Small vessel disease}.

\medterm{Costosternal chondrodynia} Alternate index label; see \textit{Costochondritis}.

\medterm{Costosternal syndrome} Alternate index label; see \textit{Costochondritis}.

\medterm{COVID-19} Alternate index label; see \textit{Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19)}.

\medterm{Crabs} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pubic lice (crabs)}.

\medterm{Cramp, muscle} Alternate index label; see \textit{Muscle cramp}.

\medterm{Cramps, menstrual} Alternate index label; see \textit{Menstrual cramps}.

\medterm{Crib death} Alternate index label; see \textit{Sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS)}.

\medterm{Crossmatch} Laboratory test mixing donor and recipient blood to detect preformed antibodies that could cause transfusion or transplant reactions.

\medterm{Cyst, Bartholin's} Alternate index label; see \textit{Bartholin's cyst}.

\medterm{Cyst, ganglion} Alternate index label; see \textit{Ganglion cyst}.

\medterm{Cyst, kidney} Alternate index label; see \textit{Kidney cysts}.

\medterm{Cyst, ovarian} Alternate index label; see \textit{Ovarian cysts}.

\medterm{Cyst, pancreatic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pancreatic cysts}.

\medterm{Cyst, pilonidal} Alternate index label; see \textit{Pilonidal cyst}.

\medterm{Cyst, spermatic} Alternate index label; see \textit{Spermatocele}.

\medterm{Cystitis, interstitial} Alternate index label; see \textit{Interstitial cystitis}.

\medterm{Cysts, epidermoid} Alternate index label; see \textit{Epidermoid cysts}.

\medterm{Cysts, sebaceous} Alternate index label; see \textit{Epidermoid cysts}.

\medterm{C-Reactive Protein} An acute-phase reactant produced by the liver in response to inflammation (IL-6). CRP
rises within 4-6 hours of inflammatory stimulus and peaks at 36-50 hours. Uses:
monitoring inflammatory disease activity (RA, vasculitis), detecting infection
postoperatively, and cardiovascular risk stratification (hs-CRP: high-sensitivity).

\medterm{C-Section} Cesarean section, surgical delivery of a fetus through incisions in the abdominal wall
and uterus.

\medterm{C. difficile} Clostridioides difficile, a gram-positive, spore-forming bacterium that causes
antibiotic-associated diarrhea and colitis. C. difficile produces toxins A and B that
cause colonic inflammation. Risk factors include antibiotic exposure, proton pump
inhibitor use, and advanced age. Treatment includes vancomycin or fidaxomicin.

\medterm{C282Y Mutation} Most common genetic cause of hereditary hemochromatosis, a C to T transition in the HFE
gene resulting in cysteine to tyrosine substitution at position 282. Homozygosity occurs
in approximately one in two hundred individuals of Northern European descent.

\medterm{CA 19-9} A blood marker that may be elevated in pancreatic and other gastrointestinal cancers as well as in benign biliary or inflammatory conditions. It is not sufficiently specific for cancer screening or diagnosis by itself.

\medterm{CA-125} A blood marker used mainly to assess some ovarian cancers and to monitor selected patients. It can also rise in
benign gynecologic, inflammatory, liver, and other malignant conditions, so it is not
sufficient by itself to screen for or diagnose ovarian cancer.

\medterm{CA125} Cancer Antigen 125, a protein tumor marker used primarily in the evaluation of ovarian
cancer.

\medterm{CABG (Coronary Artery Bypass Grafting)} Open heart surgery to bypass stenotic coronary arteries using autologous grafts.
On-pump: cardiopulmonary bypass, cardioplegic arrest. Off-pump (OPCAB): beating heart,
no bypass machine.

\synonyms
Coronary Artery Bypass Grafting

\medterm{Cabot Ring} A threadlike or ring-shaped inclusion in a red blood cell, sometimes seen in severe
anemia or disorders of erythropoiesis. It represents residual nuclear material or
mitotic spindle remnants.

\medterm{Cachexia} A multifactorial syndrome of involuntary weight loss, skeletal-muscle wasting, and
systemic inflammation associated with chronic diseases such as cancer, heart failure,
COPD, and advanced kidney disease.

\medterm{Cacosmia} A distorted or unpleasant perception of smell, in which odors may seem foul or
disagreeable. It can occur with sinonasal disease, infection, seizures, trauma, or other
neurologic disorders.

\medterm{Cadaver} A dead human body used for anatomical dissection, surgical training, and medical
research. Cadavers are preserved through embalming (arterial injection of
formaldehyde-based fixatives) to prevent decomposition and maintain tissue integrity.

\medterm{Cadaveric Spasm} Rare form of instantaneous post-mortem stiffening of a specific group of voluntary
muscles at the exact moment of death, without an intervening period of muscle
relaxation.

\medterm{Caecitis} Inflammation of the cecum. Causes include infection, inflammatory bowel disease,
ischemia, neutropenic enterocolitis, and extension of adjacent inflammatory disease.

\medterm{Caecopexy} Surgical fixation of the cecum to the abdominal wall or retroperitoneum, performed to
prevent or treat abnormal cecal mobility or recurrent cecal volvulus.

\medterm{Caecostomy} Surgical creation of an opening from the cecum to the abdominal wall for decompression
or diversion of intestinal contents. It may be temporary or part of treatment for
selected distal obstruction.

\medterm{Caecotomy} Surgical incision into the cecum, usually to remove a foreign body, access the bowel, or
facilitate another abdominal procedure.

\medterm{Caesarean Birth} An operative procedure in which a baby is delivered through an incision in the abdominal
wall and uterus. Indications: fetal distress, failure to progress in labor, breech
presentation, placenta praevia, multiple pregnancy, previous cesarean, and maternal
request.

\medterm{Cafe-au-lait Macules} Flat, uniformly hyperpigmented, light-brown to dark-brown (cafe-au-lait, "coffee with
milk") macules, > 5 mm in prepubertal children or > 15 mm in postpubertal individuals.

\medterm{Caffeine} A naturally occurring xanthine alkaloid and central nervous system stimulant found in
coffee, tea, chocolate, and many soft drinks and energy beverages. Mechanism: adenosine
receptor antagonist (primarily A1 and A2A subtypes), increasing neuronal activity and
promoting catecholamine release.

\medterm{Caffeine Withdrawal} A syndrome occurring after abrupt cessation or reduction of caffeine use. Symptoms
include headache, fatigue, depressed mood, difficulty concentrating, and flu-like
symptoms. Onset is typically 12--24 hours after last caffeine intake, peak at 20--51
hours, and duration of 2--9 days.

\medterm{Café au Lait Spot} A flat, sharply demarcated, light-brown skin macule caused by increased melanin.
Multiple lesions may be a feature of neurofibromatosis type 1 or other genetic
disorders.

\medterm{Caisson Disease} Decompression sickness caused by formation of inert-gas bubbles after a rapid reduction
in ambient pressure. It can cause joint pain, neurologic injury, respiratory symptoms,
or circulatory collapse.

\medterm{Calcaneus} Largest tarsal bone forming the heel. Articulates with talus proximally and cuboid
anteriorly. Lever for the calf muscles via the Achilles tendon on its posterior
tuberosity. Predominantly trabecular bone, the most commonly fractured tarsal bone from
falls from height. Bohler's angle assesses fractures on lateral radiographs.

\medterm{Calciferol} Vitamin D is a fat-soluble vitamin (actually a prohormone) essential for calcium and
phosphorus homeostasis and bone health. See Vitamin D: Calcitriol.

\medterm{Calcific Uremic Arteriolopathy} A rare disorder, usually associated with advanced kidney disease, in which small blood vessels calcify and cause painful skin and soft-tissue injury or necrosis.

\medterm{Calcification} Calcification is abnormal deposition of calcium salts in tissue; its significance depends on the site, pattern, underlying injury, and associated disease.
calcium or phosphate metabolism is abnormal.

\medterm{Calcinos} Cutaneous calcinosis, the deposition of insoluble calcium phosphate salts within the
skin and subcutaneous tissues, occurring in connective tissue diseases such as
scleroderma and dermatomyositis, or as a localized phenomenon. Lesions present as firm,
white, subcutaneous nodules that may ulcerate and extrude chalky material.

\medterm{Calcinosis} The pathological deposition of calcium salts in cutaneous and subcutaneous tissues,
presenting as firm, white to yellowish nodules or plaques that may ulcerate and extrude
chalky material.

\medterm{Calciphylaxis} Calcific uremic arteriolopathy causing painful skin necrosis in CKD. Risk factors
include warfarin use, elevated calcium-phosphorus product, and obesity. High mortality
rate. Treat with sodium thiosulfate, wound care, and discontinuation of warfarin. Avoid
calcium-based binders.

\medterm{Calcitonin} A 32-amino-acid peptide hormone secreted by the parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid
gland in response to elevated serum calcium. It lowers blood calcium by inhibiting
osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and increasing renal calcium excretion.

\medterm{Calcitriol and Analogues} Active vitamin D forms suppressing PTH. Calcitriol, paricalcitol, doxercalciferol.
Monitor calcium and phosphorus to avoid hypercalcemia and hyperphosphatemia. Part of
CKD-MBD management.

\medterm{Calcium} A mineral and intracellular second messenger required for bone mineralization, muscle
contraction, neurotransmitter release, blood coagulation, and enzyme regulation. Plasma
calcium is present as ionized, protein-bound, and complexed fractions.

\medterm{Calcium Blood Test} Measurement of calcium in the blood, usually reported as total calcium and interpreted
with albumin; ionized calcium may be measured directly when the biologically active
fraction is needed. It helps evaluate parathyroid disorders, bone disease, kidney
disease, and abnormal calcium levels.

\medterm{Calcium Channel Blocker Overdose} Toxic exposure to a calcium-channel blocker that may cause low blood pressure, slow heart rate, conduction abnormalities, high blood glucose, or shock. It requires urgent medical and poison-control management.

\medterm{Calcium Channel Blockers} Agents that block L-type calcium channels, reducing calcium influx into cardiac and
smooth muscle cells. Dihydropyridines (amlodipine, nifedipine) cause vasodilation and
are used for hypertension and angina.

\medterm{Calcium phosphate} The principal mineral constituent of bone and teeth, existing as hydroxyapatite
crystals. In pathological states, calcium phosphate deposits form in soft tissues
through dystrophic calcification in damaged tissue or metastatic calcification in
hypercalcemic conditions.

\medterm{Calcium Pyrophosphate Arthritis} Acute or chronic inflammatory arthritis caused by deposition of calcium pyrophosphate
crystals in cartilage and joints. Acute attacks, often called pseudogout, commonly
affect the knee or wrist and are diagnosed by identifying weakly positively birefringent
crystals in synovial fluid.

\medterm{Calcium Pyrophosphate Deposition Disease} A crystal arthropathy caused by deposition of calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystals
in articular cartilage (chondrocalcinosis). Presents with acute pseudogout (most
common): acute monoarticular inflammation of the knee or wrist, mimicking gout but less
painful.

\medterm{Calcium Sensing Receptor} G-protein coupled receptor expressed primarily on parathyroid glands and renal tubular
cells that senses extracellular ionized calcium levels. On parathyroid glands activation
suppresses PTH secretion. On renal tubular cells activation increases calcium excretion
and decreases phosphate reabsorption.

\medterm{Calcium Stones} Most common kidney stone type composed of calcium oxalate or calcium phosphate. Risk
factors include hypercalciuria, hyperoxaluria, hypocitraturia, and low urine volume.
Treat with thiazides for hypercalciuria, dietary modification, and hydration. Potassium
citrate for hypocitraturia.

\medterm{Calculus (Tartar)} Mineralized dental plaque. Cannot be removed by brushing. Requires professional scaling.
Retains plaque, promotes inflammation.

\synonyms
Tartar

\medterm{Calculus Removal} Professional cleaning to remove hardened plaque. Scaling and root planing. Ultrasonic
and hand instruments.

\medterm{Caldwell-Luc Operation} A surgical approach to the maxillary sinus through the canine fossa of the anterior
maxilla. It is used selectively for access to maxillary sinus disease, lesions, or
foreign bodies.

\medterm{Caloric Restriction} Reduced calorie intake without malnutrition. Associated with longevity, reduced chronic
disease risk. Requires careful planning.

\medterm{Calorie} A unit of energy measurement used in nutrition to quantify the energy content of foods
and energy expenditure of the body. One kilocalorie (kcal, commonly called a Calorie
with capital C) equals the energy required to raise 1 kilogram of water by 1 degree
Celsius.

\synonyms
a Calorie.

\medterm{Cambridge Classification} ERCP-based grading system for chronic pancreatitis severity evaluating the main
pancreatic duct, branches, and common bile duct. Normal pancreas shows smooth duct
without abnormalities. Equivocal shows fewer than three abnormal side branches. Mild
shows three or more abnormal side branches. Moderate shows abnormal main duct.

\medterm{Cameron Lesions} Linear gastric erosions or ulcers found on the mucosal folds at the diaphragmatic hiatus
in patients with large hiatal hernias. They result from mechanical trauma as the gastric
mucosa prolapses through the diaphragmatic crura during respiration and movement.

\medterm{Campimetry} Measurement and mapping of the visual field, usually by presenting stimuli at different
locations while the patient maintains fixation. It helps detect and characterize defects
from retinal, optic-nerve, or retrochiasmal disease.

\medterm{Camptocormia} A marked forward flexion of the trunk that increases while standing or walking and
improves when lying down. It can occur with axial myopathy, Parkinsonian disorders,
dystonia, or other neuromuscular disease.

\medterm{Camptodactyly} A congenital or acquired flexion deformity of one or more fingers, usually caused by
shortening or abnormality of tendons, muscles, fascia, or joints. It often affects the
proximal interphalangeal joints.

\medterm{Campylobacter Infection} Bacterial gastroenteritis caused most often by Campylobacter jejuni or C. coli, usually
acquired from contaminated poultry, food, water, or unpasteurized milk. It causes fever,
abdominal cramps, and diarrhea that may be bloody and can rarely precede Guillain-Barre
syndrome.

\medterm{Campylobacteriosis} Infection caused by Campylobacter species, commonly producing fever, abdominal pain, and
inflammatory diarrhea that may be bloody. Complications can include dehydration,
bacteremia, reactive arthritis, or Guillain--Barré syndrome.

\medterm{Canal of Schlemm} A circular endothelial-lined channel at the corneoscleral junction that drains aqueous
humor from the anterior chamber into the episcleral venous circulation. Impaired outflow
contributes to glaucoma.

\medterm{Canalith Repositioning Maneuver} A series of head movements used to move displaced inner-ear crystals out of a
semicircular canal, commonly to treat posterior-canal benign positional vertigo.

\medterm{Canavan Disease} An autosomal-recessive leukodystrophy caused by aspartoacylase deficiency, leading to
accumulation of N-acetylaspartate and progressive spongy degeneration of cerebral white
matter. It typically presents in infancy with hypotonia followed by developmental
impairment and macrocephaly.

\medterm{Cancer} Cancer is uncontrolled growth of abnormal cells that can invade nearby tissue and spread to distant organs; behavior and treatment depend on its tissue of origin and molecular features.
or spread to distant sites. Cancer may arise in any tissue and is classified by its site
and cell type. Early detection and treatment improve outcomes.

\medterm{Cancer Care Team} The multidisciplinary group of health professionals involved in cancer care, which may
include oncologists, surgeons, radiologists, pathologists, nurses, pharmacists,
rehabilitation specialists, psychologists, and palliative-care professionals.

\medterm{Cancer Cell} A cell that has acquired genetic and epigenetic abnormalities allowing uncontrolled
growth, impaired normal differentiation, resistance to cell death, invasion of
surrounding tissues, or spread to distant sites.

\medterm{Cancer Genetics} Inherited cancer syndromes. Examples: BRCA1/2 (breast, ovarian), Lynch syndrome (colon),
Li-Fraumeni (various).

\medterm{Cancer Nutrition} Malnutrition common in cancer. Hypermetabolism, cachexia, treatment side effects.
Nutritional support improves outcomes. High protein, high calorie.

\medterm{Cancer of Unknown Primary} Metastatic cancer in which the primary site cannot be identified despite investigation.
Workup includes histology, immunohistochemistry, and molecular profiling to guide
treatment.

\medterm{Cancer Pain} Pain caused by a tumor, its spread, or cancer treatment. It may be continuous or episodic
and can involve bone, nerves, or internal organs. Management may include treating the cancer,
non-opioid medicines, opioids, nerve-directed treatments, and supportive care.

\medterm{Cancer Registry} System for collecting cancer data. Used for epidemiology, quality improvement, research.

\medterm{Cancer Screening} Testing people without symptoms to detect certain cancers or precancerous changes early, when treatment may be more effective.

\medterm{Cancer Staging} Classification of cancer extent. TNM system: T (tumor size/extent), N (nodal
involvement), M (metastasis). Stages: 0 (in situ), I (early), II-III (advanced), IV
(metastatic). Guides treatment and prognosis.

\medterm{Cancer Surveillance} The systematic, ongoing collection and analysis of information about cancer incidence,
stage, treatment, survival, recurrence, and mortality for patient follow-up, research,
and public-health planning.

\medterm{Cancer Survivor} Person living with cancer from diagnosis through end of life and beyond.

\medterm{Cancer Susceptibility Genes} Genes in which inherited pathogenic variants increase a person’s risk of developing one
or more cancers. Examples include BRCA1, BRCA2, APC, and mismatch-repair genes; a
susceptibility variant does not guarantee that cancer will develop.

\medterm{Cancer Vaccine} A vaccine intended either to prevent cancer-causing infections or to stimulate an immune
response against cancer cells. Preventive examples target oncogenic infections, while
therapeutic vaccines are designed for selected established cancers.

\medterm{Cancer-Associated Thrombosis} Venous or arterial thrombosis occurring in the setting of active malignancy or its
treatment. Tumor biology, immobilization, surgery, central venous devices, and systemic
therapy contribute to risk; anticoagulation is individualized according to bleeding risk
and cancer factors.

\medterm{Cancrum Oris} Noma, a rapidly destructive gangrenous infection of the mouth and face that occurs
mainly in severely malnourished or immunocompromised children. It begins with gingival
or mucosal necrosis and can extend through facial tissues.

\medterm{Candida albicans infection} Infection caused by the yeast Candida albicans, ranging from mucosal disease to invasive
bloodstream or organ infection in vulnerable patients.

\medterm{Candida Auris} A multidrug-resistant yeast that can colonize skin and cause invasive bloodstream,
wound, or ear infections, especially in hospitalized or immunocompromised patients. It
spreads readily in healthcare settings and requires laboratory identification and strict
infection-control measures.

\medterm{Candidal Intertrigo} A Candida infection of skin folds (axillae, inframammary, inguinal, intergluteal,
pannus) characterized by erythematous, macerated plaques with satellite pustules at the
periphery. Most common in obese, diabetic, and immunocompromised patients, and with
prolonged antibiotic use. Presents with itching, burning, and soreness.

\medterm{Candidemia} The presence of Candida species in the bloodstream. It is an invasive fungal infection
associated with intravascular devices, surgery, intensive care, immunosuppression, and
substantial risk of organ dissemination.

\medterm{Candidiasis} Fungal infection caused by Candida species, most commonly C. albicans. Mucocutaneous:
oral thrush (white plaques on oral mucosa), esophagitis, vulvovaginitis (itching,
discharge), and intertrigo. Invasive candidiasis: candidemia and deep organ infection,
typically in immunocompromised or ICU patients. Diagnosis: culture, histopathology.

\medterm{Cane} Walking aid for balance. Types: quad (four-base), single-point. Held on opposite side of
injury.

\medterm{Canicola Fever} A form of leptospirosis associated particularly with Leptospira interrogans serovar
canicola. It may cause fever, headache, myalgia, conjunctival suffusion, and renal or
hepatic involvement.

\medterm{Canker Sore} A common aphthous ulcer of the oral mucosa, appearing as a painful, shallow, round or
oval ulcer with an erythematous border. Most heal spontaneously, but recurrent, severe,
or atypical ulcers require assessment.

\medterm{Cannabis Use Disorder} A problematic pattern of cannabis use leading to significant impairment. Criteria
include tolerance and withdrawal (irritability, anger, decreased appetite, restlessness,
anxiety, sleep disturbance). Cannabis intoxication includes euphoria, impaired judgment,
and time distortion.

\medterm{Cannot Intubate Cannot Oxygenate} The most critical airway emergency — inability to intubate the trachea and inability to
oxygenate the patient by face mask or supraglottic airway despite optimal efforts. CICO
is a life-threatening situation requiring immediate surgical airway access. Signs: SpO2
decreasing despite 100\% O2, absent end-tidal CO2, no chest rise.

\medterm{Cannulation} The insertion of a cannula into a blood vessel, body cavity, duct, or other anatomical
passage to permit drainage, infusion, monitoring, sampling, or access for a procedure.

\medterm{Cantlie Line} Functional liver division extending from the gallbladder fossa to the IVC, corresponding
to the middle hepatic vein plane. Unlike the falciform ligament (anatomical division),
it represents the true functional blood supply/biliary drainage division. Right hepatic
artery and portal vein supply the right half; left supply the left.

\medterm{Capacity} Autonomous voluntary authorization given by a competent patient to undergo a specific
medical intervention, procedure, or clinical trial. See informed consent.

\medterm{Capacity and Competence} Decision-making capacity is a clinical evaluation performed by a physician to determine
whether a patient understands the nature, consequences, risks, and alternatives of a
specific proposed medical decision at a given time.

\medterm{Capgras Syndrome} A delusional misidentification syndrome in which a person believes that someone familiar
has been replaced by an identical impostor. It can occur in neurodegenerative disease,
psychosis, brain injury, or other neurologic conditions.

\medterm{Capillaroscopy} Examination of the small vessels in the nail-fold or another accessible surface using
magnification. It can reveal capillary dilation, hemorrhage, avascular areas, and
abnormal architecture in connective-tissue and microvascular disease.

\medterm{Capillary} The smallest blood vessel in the body, forming a microscopic network (capillary bed)
that connects arterioles to venules.

\medterm{Capillary Bed} A network of interconnected capillaries that links arterioles to venules within a
tissue. It provides a large surface area for exchange of oxygen, carbon dioxide,
nutrients, hormones, and metabolic waste between blood and interstitial fluid.

\medterm{Capillary Leak Syndrome} A disorder of increased capillary permeability in which plasma and proteins move from
the intravascular space into surrounding tissues. It may cause hypotension,
haemoconcentration, hypoalbuminaemia, oedema, and shock; causes include severe
infection, immune-mediated disease, toxins, and rare idiopathic episodes.

\medterm{Capillary Refill} A bedside assessment of peripheral perfusion performed by briefly pressing a nail bed or
skin and measuring the time for normal color to return. Delayed refill may indicate poor
circulation, shock, hypothermia, or vascular disease.

\medterm{Capillary Refill Time} The time required for color to return after pressure briefly blanches a nail bed or area of skin. A delayed
result may suggest impaired peripheral perfusion, but interpretation depends on age,
temperature, technique, and the clinical setting.

\medterm{Capitate} Largest carpal bone, centrally located in the distal row as the carpal keystone. Rounded
head articulates with scaphoid and lunate; distal surface with the third metacarpal.
Last carpal bone to ossify. Wrist joint axis, bearing significant axial load. Avascular
necrosis may occur with fractures similar to scaphoid injuries.

\medterm{Capnocytophaga canimorsus} A Gram-negative, capnophilic bacillus found in the oral flora of dogs and cats that
causes severe sepsis, meningitis, and cellulitis in humans, particularly the
immunocompromised, asplenic, or elderly. Infection typically follows dog or cat bites.
It is a fastidious organism requiring prolonged incubation for culture.

\medterm{Capnography} Continuous measurement and graphical display of exhaled carbon dioxide during anesthesia
and sedation. The waveform and end-tidal value help assess ventilation, airway patency,
circulation, and confirmation of tracheal tube placement.

\medterm{Capsular Contracture} Abnormal tightening and hardening of the fibrous capsule around an implanted device,
especially a breast implant. It may cause pain, distortion, firmness, or displacement.

\medterm{Capsule Endoscopy} Non-invasive diagnostic technique using a swallowable wireless endoscopic device to
capture images of the small intestinal mucosa. The capsule contains a miniature video
camera and transmitter that sends images to a receiver worn on the patient's belt.

\medterm{Capsule of Glisson} The connective-tissue capsule surrounding the liver, which sends fibrous septa inward
along portal tracts and supports the organ's vessels, ducts, and nerves.

\medterm{Capsule of Tenon} A fascial sheath surrounding the globe of the eye and separating it from orbital fat. It
contributes to eye movement and forms a potential space used in some ophthalmic
procedures.

\medterm{Capsulectomy} Surgical removal of part or all of a fibrous capsule surrounding an organ, implant, or
other structure. The indication depends on contracture, infection, pathology, or the
need to revise a reconstruction.

\medterm{Caput Medusae} Dilated periumbilical veins radiating outward from the umbilicus, seen in portal
hypertension with recanalization of the umbilical vein. Not strictly a cardiac finding
but relevant in right heart failure causing hepatic congestion and portal hypertension.
It must be distinguished from caval collateral circulation.

\medterm{Caput Succedaneum} Edema of the scalp presenting as a soft, boggy swelling that crosses suture lines.
Caused by pressure of the vaginal walls on the fetal head during labor. Resolves within
hours to days. Differentiated from cephalohematoma (subperiosteal, does not cross suture
lines) and subgaleal hemorrhage (life-threatening, crosses suture lines).

\medterm{CAR T-Cell} Chimeric antigen receptor T-cells. Engineered immune cells targeting cancer.

\medterm{Carbapenem-Resistant Enterobacteriaceae Infection} Infection caused by Enterobacteriaceae bacteria resistant to carbapenem antibiotics,
often involving the urinary tract, lungs, bloodstream, or wounds. These infections are difficult to treat and may
spread in healthcare settings. Culture and susceptibility testing guide therapy, with strict infection-control
measures needed to prevent transmission.

\medterm{Carbapenem-resistant organisms} Bacteria that are resistant to carbapenem antibiotics, typically through production of
carbapenemases. The most important carbapenemases include KPC (Klebsiella pneumoniae
carbapenemase), NDM (New Delhi metallo-beta-lactamase), and OXA-48-like enzymes. CRO are
a major public health threat due to limited treatment options.

\medterm{Carbidopa} A peripheral dopa decarboxylase inhibitor that does not cross the blood-brain barrier;
combined with levodopa to prevent its peripheral conversion to dopamine, reducing side
effects (nausea, hypotension) and increasing CNS levodopa delivery.

\medterm{Carbohydrate} A class of organic compounds composed of carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen (general formula
Cm(H2O)n), serving as the primary energy source for the body.

\medterm{Carbohydrate Counting} Method for diabetes management. Matching insulin to carbohydrate intake. Education
essential.

\medterm{Carbolic Acid (Phenol)} An aromatic organic compound (C6H5OH) with antiseptic, disinfectant, and caustic
properties. Historically significant as the first surgical antiseptic introduced by
Joseph Lister in 1867, dramatically reducing post-operative infection and mortality.
Mechanism: denatures bacterial proteins and disrupts cell membranes.

\synonyms
Phenol

\medterm{Carbon Dioxide Absorber} A safety device used in anesthesia breathing circuits that filters out carbon dioxide
from a patient's exhaled breath, allowing the remaining anesthetic gases to be safely
recycled and rebreathed.

\medterm{Carbon Dioxide Transport} The movement of CO2 from tissues to lungs. CO2 is carried in blood in three forms:
dissolved (approximately 10 percent), bound to hemoglobin as carbamino compounds
(approximately 20 percent), and as bicarbonate (approximately 70 percent).

\medterm{Carbon Ion Therapy} Radiation using carbon ions. Higher biological effectiveness. Limited availability.

\medterm{Carbon Monoxide Poisoning} Inhibits hemoglobin oxygen binding (affinity 200x oxygen). Causes headache, dizziness,
nausea, cherry-red skin, syncope, death. Diagnosis: carboxyhemoglobin level (not
routine). Treatment: 100\% oxygen, hyperbaric oxygen if severe (neurological symptoms,
COHb >25\%, pregnancy).

\medterm{Carbonic Anhydrase Inhibitors} Medicines that inhibit carbonic anhydrase and reduce formation of aqueous humor. They
are used mainly to lower intraocular pressure and, in some circumstances, to treat
edema or metabolic disorders.

\medterm{Carboxyhemoglobin} Toxicity from inhalation of carbon monoxide (CO), a colorless, odorless gas binding
hemoglobin ~200-300 times more avidly than oxygen, forming carboxyhemoglobin (COHb) and
reducing oxygen delivery. See carbon monoxide poisoning.

\medterm{Carbuncle} A medical term describing the named biologic or clinical concept; its defining feature is the process, structure, exposure, or finding specified by the term, interpreted in context.

\medterm{Carcinogen} Substance causing cancer. Types: chemical (benzene, asbestos, aflatoxin), physical
(radiation), biological (HPV, HBV, HCV). Mechanisms: DNA damage, mutations, epigenetic
changes. IARC classifications: Group 1 (carcinogenic), Group 2A (probably carcinogenic),
Group 2B (possibly carcinogenic), Group 3 (not classifiable).

\medterm{Carcinoid Heart Disease} Cardiac complication of carcinoid syndrome occurring in approximately fifty percent of
patients with hepatic metastases. Serotonin and other vasoactive substances bypass
hepatic metabolism and cause endocardial fibrosis affecting the right-sided heart valves
particularly the tricuspid and pulmonary valves.

\medterm{Carcinoid Syndrome} Serotonin-secreting neuroendocrine tumor causing flushing, diarrhea, bronchospasm, and
right-sided heart disease. Occurs when vasoactive substances bypass hepatic metabolism
through venous drainage or liver metastases. Diagnosis is with twenty-four hour urine
5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid or serum chromogranin A.

\medterm{Carcinoid Tumor} Carcinoid tumors are well-differentiated neuroendocrine neoplasms that arise from
enterochromaffin cells throughout the gastrointestinal tract and bronchial system. The
most common primary sites are the appendix, small intestine, and rectum.

\medterm{Carcinoma} Malignant tumor of epithelial origin. Types: squamous cell carcinoma, adenocarcinoma,
basal cell carcinoma, transitional cell carcinoma. Most common cancer type (~80-90\% of
malignancies). Examples: lung carcinoma, breast carcinoma, colon carcinoma.

\medterm{Carcinoma en cuirasse} An unusual form of breast cancer characterized by diffuse, firm, indurated skin that
resembles armor. It results from extensive dermal lymphatic invasion by tumor cells.

\medterm{Carcinoma in Situ} Full-thickness dysplasia without invasion through basement membrane. Stage 0 cancer.

\medterm{Carcinosarcoma} A malignant tumor containing both carcinomatous and sarcomatous elements, most commonly
of the uterus (malignant mixed Müllerian tumor), with an aggressive course.

\medterm{Cardiac allograft} A donor heart transplanted into a recipient with end-stage heart failure. The procedure
requires lifelong immunosuppression to prevent rejection. Complications include
rejection, cardiac allograft vasculopathy, infection, and malignancy; monitored by
endomyocardial biopsy and echocardiography.

\medterm{Cardiac Amyloidosis} Deposition of amyloid protein in the heart, causing stiff ventricular walls, impaired filling,
arrhythmias, or heart failure. Important types include immunoglobulin light-chain (AL) and
transthyretin (ATTR) amyloidosis; treatment depends on the type and organ involvement.

\medterm{Cardiac Amyloidosis Diagnosis} Diagnosis involves a combination of clinical suspicion, imaging, and tissue biopsy.
Non-invasive diagnosis using Tc-99m pyrophosphate (PYP) or DPD scintigraphy for ATTR
cardiac amyloidosis (with negative serum/urine free light chains to exclude AL
amyloidosis).

\medterm{Cardiac Amyloidosis Staging} Mayo Clinic staging system for ATTR cardiac amyloidosis: Stage I (NT-proBNP <3000
pg/mL), Stage II (NT-proBNP 3000-8499), Stage III (NT-proBNP >=8500). Median survival:
Stage I: 69 months, Stage II: 47 months, Stage III: 14 months. Treatment with tafamidis
(TTR stabilizer) improves survival and functional capacity.

\medterm{Cardiac Arrest} Sudden loss of effective heart pumping, causing unresponsiveness, absent normal breathing, and no
detectable pulse. Initial rhythms may be shockable or nonshockable; treatment requires immediate
cardiopulmonary resuscitation, defibrillation when indicated, and advanced resuscitation care.

\medterm{Cardiac Arrest Survival} Outcome after cardiac arrest depends on the cause, location, initial rhythm, time to
CPR and defibrillation, and post-arrest care. Early recognition, high-quality CPR, rapid defibrillation when indicated,
advanced life support, and appropriate post-arrest treatment improve the chance of survival.

\medterm{Cardiac Axis} The mean direction of ventricular depolarization in the frontal plane, estimated from
the QRS complex on a 12-lead ECG. Left or right axis deviation may reflect conduction
disease, ventricular hypertrophy, infarction, or altered cardiac position.

\medterm{Cardiac Biomarkers} Blood measurements used to assess myocardial injury. Cardiac troponins I and T are the
preferred specific biomarkers; CK-MB may assist in selected situations, while myoglobin
is sensitive but nonspecific. Results must be interpreted with symptoms,
electrocardiography, and serial testing.

\medterm{Cardiac Catheterization} Invasive procedure involving insertion of catheters into the heart via peripheral
arteries or veins. Diagnostic catheterization measures intracardiac pressures,
saturations, and performs coronary angiography. Indications include evaluation of CAD,
valvular disease, cardiomyopathy, and congenital heart disease.

\medterm{Cardiac Conduction System} The specialized tissue responsible for generating and conducting electrical impulses
through the heart. Components include the sinoatrial (SA) node, internodal pathways,
atrioventricular (AV) node, bundle of His, right and left bundle branches, and Purkinje
fibers.

\medterm{Cardiac CT} CT imaging of heart and coronary arteries. Coronary calcium scoring, coronary CT
angiography, cardiac function assessment.

\medterm{Cardiac CT Angiography} Noninvasive imaging using CT to visualize coronary arteries and cardiac structures. It
has high negative predictive value for excluding coronary artery disease, making it
useful for low-to-intermediate risk patients with chest pain. Calcium scoring quantifies
coronary artery calcification as a marker of atherosclerosis.

\medterm{Cardiac Cycle} The sequence of events occurring during one complete heartbeat, consisting of systole
(contraction) and diastole (relaxation). During diastole, the ventricles fill with
blood; during systole, they contract and eject blood.

\medterm{Cardiac Defibrillation} Delivery of an unsynchronized electrical shock for ventricular fibrillation or pulseless
ventricular tachycardia. It is combined with high-quality CPR and advanced life support
according to the cardiac-arrest rhythm and protocol.

\medterm{Cardiac Device Infections} Infection involving a pacemaker, implantable defibrillator, generator pocket, or lead. Findings
may include local inflammation, drainage, bacteremia, or endocarditis. Evaluation uses
clinical examination, blood cultures, and imaging when indicated; treatment may require
antimicrobial therapy and removal of infected hardware.

\medterm{Cardiac Diet} A dietary pattern emphasizing vegetables, fruits, whole grains, legumes, fish,
unsaturated fats, and limited sodium, added sugars, and saturated or trans fats. It
supports cardiovascular risk reduction but should be individualized for heart failure,
kidney disease, diabetes, and nutritional adequacy.

\medterm{Cardiac Gated SPECT} SPECT imaging synchronized with ECG. Assess ventricular function, wall motion,
viability.

\medterm{Cardiac Index} Cardiac output normalized to body surface area (BSA), calculated as CO / BSA. Normal
cardiac index is 2.5-4.2 L/min/m2. It provides a more accurate assessment of cardiac
function than cardiac output alone, accounting for body size. A cardiac index below 2.2
L/min/m2 indicates cardiogenic shock.

\medterm{Cardiac Metastases} More common than primary cardiac tumors. Most frequent sources: lung cancer, breast
cancer, melanoma, lymphoma, and renal cell carcinoma. Most commonly involve the
pericardium (effusion, tamponade), but can affect myocardium and endocardium.

\medterm{Cardiac MRI} Magnetic-resonance imaging of the heart that characterizes ventricular function,
myocardial tissue, perfusion, scar, edema, infiltration, and congenital anatomy without
ionizing radiation. Gadolinium contrast is avoided or used cautiously in selected
patients with severe renal dysfunction.

\medterm{Cardiac Muscle} Involuntary, striated muscle forming the myocardium. Cardiac muscle cells are
electrically connected by intercalated discs, allowing coordinated contraction of the
heart.

\medterm{Cardiac Output} The volume of blood pumped by the heart per minute, calculated as the heart rate
multiplied by the stroke volume.

\medterm{Cardiac Output Measurement} Methods include: thermodilution via pulmonary artery catheter (bolus of cold saline and
temperature change measurement), Fick principle (O2 consumption / arteriovenous O2
difference), echocardiographic Doppler (flow velocity time integral x valve area x heart
rate), and non-invasive cardiac output monitors (bioimpedance, bioreactance).

\medterm{Cardiac Pacemaker Types} Single-chamber: pacing one chamber (atrium or ventricle). Dual-chamber (DDD): paces and
senses both atrium and ventricle, maintaining AV synchrony. Biventricular (CRT): paces
both ventricles for resynchronization in heart failure.

\medterm{Cardiac PET} Viability, perfusion, amyloidosis. Quantitative perfusion measurement.

\medterm{Cardiac PET Imaging} Positron emission tomography using radiotracers (Rb-82, N-13 ammonia, O-15 water) for
quantitative myocardial perfusion assessment. Advantages over SPECT: superior
resolution, shorter scan time, attenuation correction, and absolute myocardial blood
flow quantification (mL/g/min).

\medterm{Cardiac Radiation Injury} Late complication of mediastinal radiation therapy (Hodgkin lymphoma, breast cancer,
lung cancer). Manifestations include pericardial disease (constrictive pericarditis,
effusion), coronary artery disease (accelerated atherosclerosis), valvular disease
(fibrosis), myocardial fibrosis, and conduction abnormalities.

\medterm{Cardiac Rehab Outcomes} Cardiac rehabilitation can improve exercise capacity, cardiovascular risk-factor control,
quality of life, and psychological well-being after selected cardiac conditions. The
benefits and participation rates vary among populations and programs.

\medterm{Cardiac Rehabilitation} Supervised program for cardiac patients post-MI, PCI, CABG, heart failure. Phases:
inpatient (early mobilization), outpatient (supervised exercise, education),
maintenance. Components: exercise training, risk factor modification, psychological
support, return to work counseling. Reduces mortality, hospitalizations.

\medterm{Cardiac Resynchronization Therapy} Biventricular pacing that synchronizes contraction of the left and right ventricles in
patients with heart failure, reduced EF, and prolonged QRS duration (especially LBBB).
CRT improves symptoms, exercise capacity, and quality of life, and reduces heart failure
hospitalizations and mortality.

\medterm{Cardiac Sarcoidosis} Infiltrative disease characterized by non-caseating granulomas in the myocardium,
affecting 25-30\% of patients with systemic sarcoidosis. Presentation includes
conduction abnormalities (AV block), ventricular arrhythmias, and heart failure.

\medterm{Cardiac Skeleton} A dense connective tissue framework composed of four fibrous rings that surround the
heart valves and provide structural support. The cardiac skeleton includes the annuli
fibrosi (valve rings), the membranous septum, and the left and right fibrous trigones.

\medterm{Cardiac SPECT} A nuclear-imaging method that evaluates myocardial perfusion and can help identify ischemia, infarction,
or viable myocardium. When synchronized with the heartbeat, gated images can also assess
ventricular motion and function.

\medterm{Cardiac Stress Test} Evaluation of cardiac function and ischemia under increased cardiac workload. Exercise
stress test (treadmill, Bruce protocol): ECG monitoring for ST-segment changes,
symptoms, BP response, and arrhythmias. Sensitivity 65-75\% for CAD.

\medterm{Cardiac Stress Testing} Assessment of cardiac function under physiological or pharmacological stress to detect
ischemia. Exercise stress testing involves treadmill or bicycle exercise with continuous
ECG monitoring. Pharmacological stress (dobutamine, adenosine, regadenoson) is used when
patients cannot exercise.

\medterm{Cardiac tamponade} Cardiac tamponade occurs when fluid or blood under pressure around the heart restricts filling, reducing cardiac output and potentially causing obstructive shock.
and causing reduced circulation. It is a medical emergency.

\medterm{Cardiac Tamponade Physiology} Accumulation of pericardial fluid causing compression of cardiac chambers, impairing
diastolic filling and reducing cardiac output. Hemodynamic consequences include
equalization of diastolic pressures (RA = RV diastolic = PCWP), pulsus paradoxus, and
decreased cardiac output.

\medterm{Cardiac Tumors} Tumors involving the heart may be primary or metastatic. Primary tumors are rare; myxoma is a common benign
primary tumor and angiosarcoma is a recognized malignant type. Symptoms and treatment
depend on the tumor's location, tissue type, obstruction, embolic risk, and spread.

\medterm{Cardiac Tumors Management} Myxoma: surgical excision is curative, with low recurrence rate. Benign tumors
(rhabdomyoma, fibroma) may be observed if asymptomatic but require surgery if causing
obstruction or arrhythmia. Malignant tumors: prognosis is poor; treatment may include
surgery, chemotherapy, and radiation depending on tumor type.

\medterm{Cardio-Oncology} The subspecialty focusing on cardiovascular care of cancer patients, addressing cancer
therapy-related cardiac dysfunction, prevention, and management. Key concerns include
anthracycline cardiotoxicity, trastuzumab-related cardiomyopathy, immune checkpoint
inhibitor myocarditis, and radiation-induced heart disease.

\medterm{Cardiogenic Shock} A state of inadequate tissue perfusion caused by severe cardiac dysfunction. It may produce
low blood pressure, altered mental status, cool extremities, reduced urine output, metabolic acidosis, or other signs of
organ hypoperfusion.

\medterm{Cardiogenic Shock Management} Emergency management of shock caused by severe cardiac dysfunction. It includes
support of oxygenation and circulation, treatment of the underlying cause, and selected use of vasopressors, inotropes,
mechanical support, or revascularization.

\medterm{Cardiology} The branch of medicine devoted to the study, diagnosis, and treatment of disorders of
the heart and circulatory system. Cardiologists manage conditions including coronary
artery disease, heart failure, valvular heart disease, arrhythmias, cardiomyopathy,
pericardial disease, congenital heart disease, and hypertension.

\medterm{Cardiomegaly} Abnormal enlargement of the heart, identified clinically, on chest radiography, or with
cardiac imaging. It may reflect chamber dilatation, myocardial hypertrophy, pericardial
effusion, or a combination of these; causes include hypertension, valvular disease,
cardiomyopathy, congenital heart disease, and high-output states.

\medterm{Cardiomyopathy} Cardiomyopathy is disease of the heart muscle that can impair contraction, relaxation, rhythm, or valve function and may lead to heart failure or sudden death.
disease, valvular disease, and congenital heart disease as primary causes. The three
main types are dilated, hypertrophic, and restrictive cardiomyopathy.

\medterm{Cardiomyopathy Classification} The MOGE(S) classification system provides phenotypic and genotypic description: M
(morpho-functional phenotype), O (organ/system involvement), G (genetic inheritance), E
(etiology), S (functional status/NYHA class).

\medterm{Cardioplegia} Deliberate temporary arrest and protection of the heart during cardiac surgery, usually
achieved with a cold or blood-based solution that alters myocardial electrical and
metabolic activity.

\medterm{Cardiopulmonary Exercise Testing} Comprehensive assessment of cardiovascular and pulmonary function during graded exercise
on a treadmill or cycle ergometer. Measures: VO2 max, anaerobic threshold, ventilatory
efficiency (VE/VCO2 slope), and hemodynamic responses.

\medterm{Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation} A sequence of chest compressions, ventilation, defibrillation when indicated, and
advanced life-support interventions for cardiac arrest. Its purpose is to maintain
circulation and oxygen delivery until spontaneous circulation is restored or definitive
treatment is provided.

\medterm{Cardiorenal Syndrome} Dysfunction of the heart and kidneys where acute or chronic failure of one organ
contributes to dysfunction of the other. Type 1: acute cardiac dysfunction causing acute
kidney injury (e.g., cardiogenic shock). Type 2: chronic cardiac dysfunction causing
progressive CKD. Type 3: acute kidney injury causing cardiac dysfunction.

\medterm{Cardioselective Beta-Blockers} Cardioselective beta-blockers selectively block beta-1 adrenergic receptors, which are
predominant in the heart, while having minimal effects on beta-2 receptors in the
bronchial smooth muscle and peripheral vasculature at therapeutic doses. Common
cardioselective beta-blockers include metoprolol, atenolol, bisoprolol, and nebivolol.

\medterm{Cardiospasm} Spasm or impaired relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter, historically used as a
term for achalasia. It causes dysphagia, regurgitation, and sometimes chest discomfort.

\medterm{CTG} Abbreviation for cardiotocography; continuous electronic monitoring of fetal heart rate and uterine contractions during labour.

\synonyms
Cardiotocography

\medterm{Cardiotocography} A continuous electronic recording of the fetal heart rate and uterine contractions used
to assess fetal well-being during pregnancy and labor. The CTG machine uses two
transducers placed on the maternal abdomen: an ultrasound transducer records the fetal
heart rate, and a tocodynamometer records uterine contractions.

\medterm{Cardiotoxicity} Injury or functional impairment of the heart caused by a toxic exposure, systemic
disease, or treatment. Manifestations include arrhythmia, myocardial dysfunction,
ischemia, or heart failure.

\medterm{Cardiotoxicity from Chemotherapy} Chemotherapy agents that damage the myocardium, most commonly anthracyclines
(doxorubicin, daunorubicin). Cardiotoxicity presents as dilated cardiomyopathy with
reduced ejection fraction. Risk is dose-dependent (doxorubicin cumulative dose >550
mg/m2).

\medterm{Cardiovascular Changes in Pregnancy} Plasma volume increases 40-50\% (total volume ~5 L), red cell mass increases 20-30\%
(physiologic anemia of pregnancy due to dilution). Cardiac output increases 30-50\% by
32 weeks (stroke volume increases initially, then heart rate).

\medterm{Cardiovascular disease} Disease affecting the heart or blood vessels, including coronary artery disease, heart
failure, arrhythmias, valvular disease, and vascular disorders.

\medterm{Cardiovascular Health} Cardio is shorthand for cardiovascular health or exercise that elevates heart rate and
respiratory effort to strengthen the heart, lungs, and circulatory system. Aerobic
activities such as walking, running, cycling, and swimming improve cardiac output, lower
blood pressure, and enhance metabolic efficiency.

\medterm{Cardiovascular Screening} Evaluation of cardiovascular risk or disease in people with or without symptoms. It may
include blood-pressure measurement, lipid testing, diabetes assessment, and targeted
testing based on age and risk.

\medterm{Cardiovascular System} The organ system comprising the heart and blood vessels (arteries, arterioles,
capillaries, venules, veins) responsible for circulating blood throughout the body.

\medterm{Cardioversion} A medical term describing the named biologic or clinical concept; its defining feature is the process, structure, exposure, or finding specified by the term, interpreted in context.

\medterm{Cardioversion (Synchronized)} Delivery of an electrical shock synchronized with the QRS complex to restore a
more organized cardiac rhythm. It is used for selected tachyarrhythmias, with energy,
sedation, anticoagulation, and monitoring determined by the rhythm and the patient’s
clinical condition.

\synonyms
Synchronized

\medterm{Carditis} Inflammation of the heart or one of its layers, including the myocardium, endocardium,
or pericardium. It may result from infection, immune-mediated disease, or systemic
inflammation.

\medterm{Care Coordination} Deliberate organization and communication of healthcare activities among professionals,
services, patients, and caregivers to support safe, continuous, and appropriate care
across settings.

\medterm{Caregiver} A person who provides assistance with another person’s health care, daily activities, or support. A caregiver may be a family member, friend, or paid professional.

\medterm{Caregiver Burden} The emotional, physical, social, or financial strain associated with providing ongoing care for another person.

\medterm{Caregiver Stress} Emotional, physical, social, or financial strain associated with providing ongoing care for another person.

\medterm{Caries} Progressive destruction of calcified tooth tissue caused by acid-producing microbial
biofilm acting on fermentable carbohydrates. Untreated disease can extend from enamel
into dentin and pulp.

\medterm{Caries (Dental)} A biofilm-mediated, sugar-driven, multifactorial disease causing net demineralization of
enamel, dentin, or cementum. Risk is influenced by fermentable-carbohydrate frequency,
fluoride exposure, saliva, oral hygiene, tooth anatomy, and previous disease.

\synonyms
Dental

\medterm{Caries Risk Assessment} Systematic evaluation of an individual's likelihood of developing dental caries. Low
risk: no active caries, good oral hygiene, regular fluoride exposure. Moderate risk: one
or more active lesions in past 3 years, fair oral hygiene.

\medterm{Carina} The ridge at the base of the trachea where it bifurcates into the right and left main
bronchi. It is a landmark for endotracheal tube placement and bronchoscopy. The right
main bronchus is wider, shorter, and more vertical than the left, making it the
preferred path for aspirated foreign bodies.

\medterm{Cariogenic} Capable of promoting dental caries, especially by producing acid from fermentable
carbohydrates or supporting a plaque environment that demineralizes tooth structure.

\medterm{Cariogram} A computer-based program that illustrates the interaction of caries-related factors and
provides an overall caries risk assessment. Factors: diet (content, frequency), bacteria
(mutans streptococci, lactobacilli), fluoride exposure, saliva (flow rate, buffering
capacity), plaque amount, and clinical/social history.

\medterm{Carney Complex} Autosomal dominant multiple endocrine neoplasia syndrome (NAME syndrome / LAMB
syndrome), caused by PRKAR1A (17q22-24, protein kinase A regulatory subunit type 1A,
70\% of cases) or PRKACA locus.

\medterm{Carnitine} Transports long-chain fatty acids into mitochondria for beta-oxidation. Deficiency
causes hypoketotic hypoglycemia.

\medterm{Caroli Disease} Congenital, autosomal recessive fibropolycystic liver disease (PKHD1 gene, encodes
fibrocystin/polyductin; same gene as infantile polycystic kidney disease) characterized
by non-obstructive, segmental, saccular dilation of the larger intrahepatic bile ducts.

\medterm{Carotid Artery} The paired arterial system supplying the head and neck. Each common carotid artery
divides into internal and external carotid arteries; the internal carotid supplies the
brain and orbit, while the external carotid supplies most external head and neck
structures.

\medterm{Carotid Artery Disease} Narrowing or blockage of the carotid arteries, most often caused by atherosclerotic
plaque. It can reduce cerebral blood flow or release emboli and is an important cause of
transient ischemic attack and ischemic stroke.

\medterm{Carotid Artery Stenting} Endovascular placement of a stent across a carotid stenosis, usually with
embolic-protection strategies, to improve cerebral blood flow and reduce stroke risk in
selected patients. Antiplatelet therapy and peri-procedural neurologic monitoring are
required.

\medterm{Carotid Bruit} An audible sound over a carotid artery caused by turbulent blood flow. It may occur with carotid
atherosclerosis but does not reliably indicate the severity of stenosis; suspected disease is
assessed with appropriate vascular imaging and risk evaluation.

\medterm{Carotid cavernous fistula} An abnormal communication between the carotid artery or its branches and the cavernous
sinus, most commonly traumatic but also spontaneous. Clinical features include pulsatile
proptosis, chemosis, bruit over the orbit, and cranial nerve palsies. Diagnosis is by CT
or MR angiography.

\medterm{Carotid cavernous sinus fistula} An abnormal connection between the carotid arterial system and the cavernous sinus,
which may cause eye redness, pulsation, proptosis, or impaired vision.

\medterm{Carotid Duplex} Ultrasound combining B-mode and Doppler imaging of carotid arteries. Assesses stenosis,
plaque characterization.

\medterm{Carotid Duplex Ultrasound} Ultrasound evaluation of the carotid arteries for atherosclerotic plaque and stenosis.
Measures peak systolic velocity (PSV) and end-diastolic velocity (EDV) in the internal
carotid artery (ICA).

\medterm{Carotid Endarterectomy} Surgical removal of atherosclerotic plaque from a carotid artery to reduce stroke risk
in selected patients. Suitability depends on symptoms, degree of narrowing, operative risk, life expectancy, and current
clinical guidance.

\medterm{Carotid Stenosis} Narrowing of a carotid artery, usually caused by atherosclerosis. It can reduce blood flow
to the brain or serve as a source of emboli. Evaluation and treatment depend on symptoms, degree of narrowing, imaging,
and overall stroke risk.

\medterm{Carpal Tunnel} Narrow passageway on the palmar wrist, bounded by carpal bones dorsally and the flexor
retinaculum as the roof. Transmits the median nerve and flexor tendons. Carpal tunnel
syndrome causes numbness in the lateral 3.5 digits and thenar weakness.

\medterm{Carpal Tunnel Syndrome} The most common entrapment neuropathy, caused by compression of the median nerve as it
passes through the carpal tunnel, a narrow fibro-osseous canal bounded by the carpal
bones and the flexor retinaculum (transverse carpal ligament) at the wrist.

\medterm{Carpal Tunnel Syndrome Tests} Provocative tests for diagnosing carpal tunnel syndrome. Tinel sign: percuss over median
nerve at the carpal tunnel tingling in median distribution. Phalen sign: hold wrists in
full flexion for 60 seconds reproduces symptoms. Durkan test (carpal compression):
direct compression over carpal tunnel for 30 seconds paresthesias.

\medterm{Carrier} Individual heterozygous for a recessive genetic disorder. Typically asymptomatic but can
pass mutant allele to offspring. Carrier screening recommended for certain populations
(e.g., Ashkenazi Jewish for Tay-Sachs).

\medterm{Carrier State} Asymptomatic infection with pathogen capable of transmission. Examples: typhoid carrier,
hepatitis B carrier, meningococcal carrier. Importance: public health control,
screening, treatment.

\medterm{Cartilage} A tough, flexible avascular connective tissue found in joints, the ear, nose, rib cage,
and intervertebral discs. Composed of chondrocytes embedded in an extracellular matrix
of collagen fibers, proteoglycans, and water, providing resilience and compressive
strength.

\medterm{Carvedilol} A nonselective beta-blocker with additional alpha-1 adrenergic blockade and antioxidant
properties, producing vasodilation with balanced reduction of heart rate, contractility,
and afterload.

\medterm{Case Definition} Standardized clinical, laboratory, and epidemiologic criteria used to classify a person
as a suspected, probable, or confirmed case for surveillance or outbreak investigation.
It may differ from the criteria used for individual clinical diagnosis.

\medterm{Case Fatality Rate} Proportion of cases dying from disease. Measures severity.

\medterm{Case Management} Coordinated planning and follow-up of health and social services to help a person obtain appropriate
care, avoid duplication, and meet clinical and functional goals.

\medterm{Case-Control Study} Observational study comparing cases with disease to controls without. Looks back at
exposures.

\medterm{Case-Fatality Rate} Proportion of cases dying from disease. Formula: deaths / cases. Measures severity.

\medterm{Caseous Necrosis} A distinctive form of cell death that characteristically occurs in granulomatous
infections, most notably tuberculosis. The term derives from the cheese-like, friable,
whitish-yellow appearance of the necrotic tissue on gross examination.

\medterm{Castleman Disease} Heterogeneous group of lymphoproliferative disorders (angiofollicular lymph node
hyperplasia, giant lymph node hyperplasia) sharing lymph node histopathology.

\medterm{Cat Scratch Disease} A regional lymphadenitis caused by Bartonella henselae, a gramnegative bacillus
transmitted to humans through cat scratches, bites, or licks on broken skin,
particularly from kittens (which carry higher bacterial loads).

\medterm{Cat-Scratch Disease} An infection caused by Bartonella henselae, usually transmitted by a scratch or bite
from an infected cat. It commonly causes a papule followed by regional lymph-node
enlargement, fever, and fatigue; atypical disease may affect the eye, liver, spleen, or
nervous system.

\medterm{Cat-Scratch Fever} A usually self-limited infection caused by Bartonella henselae, commonly producing a papule at the
inoculation site and regional lymph-node enlargement.

\medterm{Catabolism} The set of metabolic pathways that break down complex molecules into simpler units,
releasing energy captured as ATP, NADH, FADH2, NADPH. Examples: glycolysis,
beta-oxidation, proteolysis, glycogenolysis, nucleotide catabolism.

\medterm{Catalepsy} A state of markedly reduced movement and responsiveness in which a person may maintain an imposed
posture. It can occur in catatonia and other neurologic or psychiatric conditions.

\medterm{Catalyst} A substance that increases the rate of a chemical reaction without being consumed, by
lowering the activation energy (providing an alternative reaction pathway). Enzymes are
biological catalysts (proteins or ribozymes). Catalysts do not alter the reaction
equilibrium ( G), only the kinetics.

\medterm{Cataract} A cataract is an opacity or clouding of the crystalline lens that impairs the
transmission of light to the retina, resulting in blurred vision, glare, decreased
contrast sensitivity, and progressive vision loss, and it is the leading cause of
preventable blindness worldwide.

\medterm{Catastrophic Aging} Rapid functional decline after stressor (illness, surgery, fall). Elderly have decreased
reserve, less ability to recover. Prevention: comprehensive assessment, optimization,
early intervention.

\medterm{Catatonia} A neuropsychiatric syndrome characterized by motor abnormalities including immobility,
mutism, posturing, waxy flexibility, stereotypy, mannerisms, grimacing, echolalia, and
echopraxia. It can occur in mood disorders, psychotic disorders, medical conditions, or
as catatonia due to another medical condition.

\medterm{Catch-Up Immunization} Vaccination of a person who is behind the recommended schedule using age- and
risk-appropriate intervals. Doses generally do not need to be restarted because of
delay, but minimum ages and intervals must be respected.

\medterm{Catecholamine Crisis} Acute life-threatening elevation of catecholamines causing severe hypertension,
tachycardia, and multi-organ dysfunction. Occurs in pheochromocytoma and paraganglioma
precipitated by anesthesia, surgery, trauma, or medications that stimulate catecholamine
release.

\medterm{Catecholamines (Urine/Plasma)} Measurement of catecholamines or their metabolites in blood or urine. These
tests help evaluate suspected pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma, but results must be interpreted with medications,
stress, sampling conditions, and clinical findings.

\synonyms
Urine/Plasma

\medterm{Category I Fetal Heart Tracing} A normal fetal heart rate tracing by NICHD 2008 criteria. All three characteristics
present: baseline 110-160 bpm, moderate variability (6-25 bpm), and no late or variable
decelerations. May have accelerations. Category I tracings are associated with normal
fetal acid-base status.

\medterm{Category II Fetal Heart Tracing} An indeterminate fetal heart rate tracing that falls between Category I and III.

\medterm{Category III Fetal Heart Tracing} An abnormal fetal heart rate tracing indicating potential fetal hypoxia or acidosis. Two
specific patterns: (1) absent baseline variability with recurrent late decelerations,
(2) absent baseline variability with recurrent variable decelerations, and (3)
sinusoidal pattern.

\medterm{Catheter} Flexible tube for drainage or infusion. Types: urinary (Foley), intravenous, central
venous, arterial. Materials: silicone, latex, PVC.

\medterm{Catheter-Associated UTI} Urinary tract infection related to indwelling urinary catheter. Most common
healthcare-associated infection. Risk: prolonged catheterization, female sex, diabetes,
immunosuppression. Prevention: avoid unnecessary catheters, aseptic insertion, closed
drainage, daily hygiene, early removal.

\medterm{Catheter-Related Infections} Infections caused by microorganisms entering along or contaminating an intravascular
or other indwelling catheter. They may cause local inflammation or bloodstream infection;
removal or replacement and antimicrobial treatment depend on the catheter and severity.

\medterm{Catheterization (Urinary)} Insertion of a flexible tube (catheter) through the urethra or suprapubically into the
urinary bladder to drain urine. Types: Foley catheter (indwelling balloon catheter),
intermittent (in-out) catheter, and suprapubic catheter.

\synonyms
Urinary

\medterm{Cauda Equina Syndrome} A neurosurgical emergency caused by compression of the cauda equina nerve roots below
the level of the conus medullaris (typically at L1-L2 and below), resulting in bilateral
lower extremity weakness, sensory loss, and bladder and bowel dysfunction.

\medterm{Caudal} Positioned toward the tail or lower part of the body.

\medterm{Cauliflower Ear} A permanent deformity of the outer ear caused by blood or fluid collecting between the cartilage and its covering,
usually after blunt trauma or repeated friction. Untreated separation can deprive cartilage of blood supply and
produce a thickened, irregular ear. Prompt drainage and compression reduce the risk of deformity.

\medterm{Cause of Death} The specific disease, injury, or toxic agent responsible for initiating the lethal
physiological sequence leading directly to death (e.g., Gunshot wound to the head, Acute
myocardial infarction).

\medterm{Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis} Life-threatening septic thrombosis of the cavernous sinus, usually a complication of
mid-face, nasal, paranasal sinus (sphenoid, ethmoid), dental, or orbital infection
spreading through the valveless ophthalmic veins.

\medterm{CD4 Count} The number of CD4-positive T-helper lymphocytes in a blood sample. It helps assess immune function and risk of
opportunistic infection in people with HIV, but interpretation depends on trends, viral load,
clinical findings, and current treatment guidance.

\medterm{CD4-Positive T Cell} A T lymphocyte that recognizes peptide presented by major histocompatibility complex
class II molecules. Its differentiated subsets coordinate immune responses, including
macrophage activation, B-cell help, mucosal defense, and immune regulation.

\medterm{CD8-Positive T Cell} A T lymphocyte that recognizes peptide presented by major histocompatibility complex
class I molecules. Cytotoxic CD8-positive cells can induce apoptosis in infected,
malignant, or otherwise altered target cells.

\medterm{CEA (Carcinoembryonic Antigen)} A blood tumor marker used mainly to monitor selected colorectal and other cancers. Levels can
be affected by smoking, benign disease, and other malignancies; changes are interpreted
with imaging, symptoms, and the clinical context rather than a single cutoff.

\synonyms
Carcinoembryonic Antigen

\medterm{Cecal volvulus} Axial twisting of the cecum and ascending colon around its mesentery, accounting for
approximately one-third of colonic volvulus cases. It presents with acute or subacute
large bowel obstruction, abdominal pain, and distension. Diagnosis is by CT scan showing
the characteristic whirl sign and a massively dilated cecum.

\medterm{Cecum} First and widest large intestinal part in the right iliac fossa below the ileocecal
valve, a blind-ended pouch approximately 6 cm long. Appendix arises from its
posteromedial wall approximately 2 cm below the valve, containing abundant lymphoid
tissue. Cecal volvulus causes large bowel obstruction.

\medterm{Ceftazidime-avibactam} A combination of the third-generation cephalosporin ceftazidime with the non-beta-lactam
beta-lactamase inhibitor avibactam. Avibactam inhibits class A, class C, and some class
D beta-lactamases, including KPC carbapenemases. This combination has activity against
many carbapenem-resistant Enterobacteriaceae and Pseudomonas aeruginosa.

\medterm{Celiac Artery} The first major ventral branch of the abdominal aorta at T12, arising from the aorta
just below the diaphragm and supplying the foregut (stomach, proximal duodenum, liver,
gallbladder, spleen, pancreas). It trifurcates into the left gastric, splenic, and
common hepatic arteries, with rich collateral circulation between these branches.

\medterm{Celiac Crisis} Acute life-threatening presentation of celiac disease characterized by severe
dehydration, electrolyte imbalances, metabolic acidosis, hypocalcemic tetany, and
hypovolemic shock. Most commonly occurs in infants and young children after a viral
illness or introduction of gluten-containing foods.

\medterm{Celiac Disease} An immune-mediated enteropathy triggered by gluten ingestion in genetically predisposed
individuals (HLA-DQ2/DQ8). Gliadin peptides from wheat, barley, and rye activate an
autoimmune response against tissue transglutaminase (tTG), causing villous atrophy,
crypt hyperplasia, and intraepithelial lymphocytosis in the small intestine.

\medterm{Celiac Trunk} First major abdominal aortic branch at T12-L1, the foregut artery. Divides into left
gastric (lesser curvature), splenic (spleen, pancreas, greater curvature), and common
hepatic (proper hepatic/gastroduodenal) arteries. Surrounded by the celiac plexus.

\medterm{Celiac Trunk Stenosis} Narrowing of the celiac trunk typically caused by extrinsic compression from the median
arcuate ligament of the diaphragm, known as median arcuate ligament syndrome or celiac
artery compression syndrome. Causes chronic abdominal pain particularly after eating and
weight loss due to postprandial mesenteric ischemia.

\medterm{Cell} The fundamental structural and functional unit of all living organisms. Human cells are
eukaryotic, characterized by a nucleus containing DNA and membrane-bound organelles.

\medterm{Cell-Free Fetal DNA Screening} A prenatal screening test that analyzes placental cell-free DNA in maternal blood
to estimate the chance of selected chromosomal conditions. It is usually offered from
early pregnancy onward. It is a screening test, not a diagnostic test, and a positive or
inconclusive result requires appropriate counseling and follow-up.

\medterm{Cell-mediated immunity} An immune response mediated mainly by T lymphocytes and other cells rather than by antibodies. It is important in defense against intracellular pathogens and in immune surveillance.

\medterm{Cellular Adaptation} Cellular adaptations are reversible changes in cell morphology and function that occur
in response to altered physiological demands or mild pathological conditions.
